order diffs + commitment depth.\"\"\"\n    d_ll = (np.asarray(b[\"LL_ownfirst\"], float)\n            - np.asarray(b[\"LL_antifirst\"], float)).mean(axis=0)\n    d_t = (np.asarray(b[\"T_ownfirst\"], float)\n           - np.asarray(b[\"T_antifirst\"], float)).mean(axis=0)\n\n    def commit_layer(prof: np.ndarray) -> int | None:\n        pos = prof > 0\n        for li in range(len(prof)):\n            if pos[li:].all():\n                return li\n        return None\n\n    return {\"gate\": \"OR2\", \"advisory\": True,\n            \"D_LL_profile\": [round(float(x), 4) for x in d_ll],\n            \"D_T_profile\": [round(float(x), 4) for x in d_t],\n            \"commit_layer_LL\": commit_layer(d_ll),\n            \"commit_layer_T\": commit_layer(d_t)}\n\n\ndef _or3(b: dict, rng: np.random.Generator, n_null: int = N_NULL) -> dict:\n    \"\"\"RECENCY KERNEL secondary: L(anti@slot0) - L(anti@slot3) at final.\n    >0 sig = trailing contradiction hurts most (within-arm recency).\"\"\"\n    band = b[\"band_idx\"]\n    early = np.asarray(b[\"LL_slot\"], float)[:, 0, -1]\n    late = np.asarray(b[\"LL_slot\"], float)[:, -1, -1]\n    obs = float((early - late).mean())\n    null = paired_permutation(early, late, rng, n_iter=n_null)\n    g = gate(obs, null, \"greater\", ALPHA, \"OR3_recency_kernel\",\n             claim_register=Register.value, probe_register=Register.value)\n    slot_curve = [float(np.asarray(b[\"LL_slot\"], float)[:, j, -1].mean())\n                  for j in range(np.asarray(b[\"LL_slot\"]).shape[1])]\n    t_early = _band_mean(np.asarray(b[\"T_slot\"], float)[:, 0, :], band)\n    t_late = _band_mean(np.asarray(b[\"T_slot\"], float)[:, -1, :], band)\n    return {\"gate\": \"OR3\", \"secondary\": True, \"kernel\": obs, \"p\": g.p,\n            \"pass\": bool(g.verdict),\n            \"slot_curve_L_final\": slot_curve,\n            \"T_band_kernel_advisory\": float((t_early - t_late).mean())}\n\n\ndef verdict(or0: dict, or1: dict) -> str:\n    if not or0[\"anchor_ok\"]:\n        return \"VOID\"\n    if not or0[\"replication_ok\"]:\n        return \"SPLIT-NOT-REPLICATED\"\n    if or1[\"A_neg\"] and or1[\"B_pos\"]:\n        return \"DEPTH-COMMITMENT\"\n    if or1[\"A_pos\"] and or1[\"B_neg\"]:\n        return \"REGISTER-DISSOCIATION\"\n    return \"ENTANGLED-PARTIAL\"\n\n\ndef compute_gates(b: dict, rng: np.random.Generator,\n                  n_null: int = N_NULL) -> dict:\n    or0 = _or0(b, rng, n_null)\n    or1 = _or1(b, rng, n_null)\n    or2 = _or2(b)\n    or3 = _or3(b, rng, n_null)\n    return {\"verdict\": verdict(or0, or1), \"a_priori\": APRIORI,\n            \"gates\": {\"OR0\": or0, \"OR1\": or1, \"OR2\": or2, \"OR3\": or3}}\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# --validate: planted worlds (no model)\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n_NL, _N = 36, 24\n\n\ndef _world(rng, kind: str) -> dict:\n    nl, n = _NL, _N\n    band = ti.band_layers(nl)\n    metric = {\"real_margin\": 2.5, \"per_class_ok\": True,\n              \"ident_mean\": 0.005, \"ident_max\": 0.02}\n\n    def base(level=0.5):\n        return level + rng.normal(0.0, 0.08, (n, nl))\n\n    def with_profile(anti: np.ndarray, prof: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n        return anti + prof[None, :] + rng.normal(0.0, 0.06, (n, nl))\n\n    flat = lambda v: np.full(nl, float(v))                    # noqa: E731\n    late_flip = np.where(np.arange(nl) >= int(0.90 * nl), 0.9, -0.8)\n\n    # kernel slots: recency (trailing hurts most) unless overridden\n    slot_levels = np.array([1.5, 0.8, 0.3, -0.1])\n\n    if kind == \"depth_commitment\":\n        d_ll, d_t = late_flip, late_flip\n    elif kind == \"register_dissociation\":\n        d_ll, d_t = flat(0.8), flat(-0.8)\n        slot_levels = np.array([0.5, 0.5, 0.5, 0.5])          # flat kernel\n    elif kind == \"entangled\":\n        d_ll = np.where(np.arange(nl) >= int(0.90 * nl), 0.9, 0.0)  # A ns\n        d_t = flat(-0.8)                                       # B neg\n    elif kind == \"split_not_replicated\":\n        d_ll, d_t = flat(0.8), flat(0.5)                       # T band wrong sign\n    elif kind == \"void\":\n        d_ll, d_t = late_flip, late_flip\n        metric = {\"real_margin\": -0.3, \"per_class_ok\": False,\n                  \"ident_mean\": 0.4, \"ident_max\": 2.0}\n    else:\n        raise ValueError(kind)\n\n    ll_anti, t_anti = base(0.2), base(-0.2)\n    ll_slot = np.stack([1.0 + lv + rng.normal(0.0, 0.06, (n, nl))\n                        for lv in slot_levels], axis=1)\n    t_slot = np.stack([0.5 * lv + rng.normal(0.0, 0.06, (n, nl))\n                       for lv in slot_levels], axis=1)\n    return {\"LL_own_only\": base(2.0), \"LL_filler\": base(1.8),\n            \"LL_inter\": base(0.8),\n            \"LL_ownfirst\": with_profile(ll_anti, d_ll), \"LL_antifirst\": ll_anti,\n            \"T_ownfirst\": with_profile(t_anti, d_t), \"T_antifirst\": t_anti,\n            \"LL_slot\": ll_slot, \"T_slot\": t_slot,\n            \"band_idx\": band, \"metric\": metric}\n\n\ndef run_validate() -> int:\n    print(\"── §P-ORDER-RECONCILE --validate (planted worlds, no model) ──\")\n    want = {\"depth_commitment\": \"DEPTH-COMMITMENT\",\n            \"register_dissociation\": \"REGISTER-DISSOCIATION\",\n            \"entangled\": \"ENTANGLED-PARTIAL\",\n            \"split_not_replicated\": \"SPLIT-NOT-REPLICATED\",\n            \"void\": \"VOID\"}\n    kernel_want = {\"depth_commitment\": True, \"register_dissociation\": False}\n    ok = True\n    for kind, expect_v in want.items():\n        rng = np.random.default_rng(hash(kind) % (2**31))\n        res = compute_gates(_world(rng, kind), rng, n_null=2000)\n        good = res[\"verdict\"] == expect_v\n        if kind in kernel_want:\n            good &= res[\"gates\"][\"OR3\"][\"pass\"] == kernel_want[kind]\n        ok &= good\n        g = res[\"gates\"]\n        print(f\"  {kind:22s} -> {res['verdict']:22s} expect {expect_v:22s} \"\n              f\"[A={g['OR1']['A_LL_band']:+.2f} B={g['OR1']['B_T_final']:+.2f} \"\n              f\"OR3={g['OR3']['pass']}] {'✓' if good else '✗ FAIL'}\")\n\n    # ── construction primitives ──\n    w, c = \"wug\", 0\n    # (1) slot arms are content-matched multisets (only slot differs)\n    slots = [slot_prefix(w, c, s) for s in range(N_SLOTS)]\n    stmt_sets = [tuple(sorted(p.strip().split(\". \"))) for p in slots]\n    prim1 = len(set(stmt_sets)) == 1 and len(set(slots)) == N_SLOTS\n    ok &= prim1\n    print(f\"  primitive slot content-match      {'✓' if prim1 else '✗ FAIL'}\")\n\n    # (2) trailing-anti slot == s328 ownfirst k_anti=1 (construction identity)\n    prim2 = slot_prefix(w, c, K_OWN) == ts.mix_ownfirst_prefix(w, c, 1)\n    ok &= prim2\n    print(f\"  primitive slot3 ≡ ownfirst(k=1)   {'✓' if prim2 else '✗ FAIL'}\")\n\n    # (3) balanced arms are the literal s328 builders (content-matched pair)\n    of = ARM_BUILDERS[\"ownfirst_bal\"](w, c)\n    af = ARM_BUILDERS[\"antifirst\"](w, c)\n    prim3 = (sorted(of.strip().split(\". \")) == sorted(af.strip().split(\". \"))\n             and of != af)\n    ok &= prim3\n    print(f\"  primitive balanced content-match  {'✓' if prim3 else '✗ FAIL'}\")\n\n    # (4) band excludes the final layer (the crossing has two depths)\n    band = ti.band_layers(_NL)\n    prim4 = max(band) < _NL - 1 and min(band) > 0\n    ok &= prim4\n    print(f\"  primitive band ≠ final depth      {'✓' if prim4 else '✗ FAIL'}\")\n\n    print(f\"\\n── --validate {'ALL PASS' if ok else 'FAIL'} ──\")\n    return 0 if ok else 1\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# Model path\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\ndef run_model(args) -> int:\n    import torch\n    import torch.nn.functional as F\n    from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM, AutoTokenizer\n\n    from verbum import jlens\n\n    dev = (args.device if (args.device != \"mps\"\n                           or torch.backends.mps.is_available()) else \"cpu\")\n    rng = np.random.default_rng(args.seed)\n    tok = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(args.model_id)\n    if tok.pad_token is None:\n        tok.pad_token = tok.eos_token\n    model = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(\n        args.model_id, dtype=getattr(torch, args.dtype)).to(dev).eval()\n    for p in model.parameters():\n        p.requires_grad_(False)\n    nl = jlens.n_layers(model)\n    band = ti.band_layers(nl)\n    out_dir = Path(args.out)\n    out_dir.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)\n    print(f\"[or] {args.model_id} dev={dev} n_layers={nl} \"\n          f\"band=L{band[0]}..L{band[-1]} final=L{nl - 1} \"\n          f\"slots={N_SLOTS}\", flush=True)\n\n    # ── direct-logits surprisal (s328 instrument, for anchor + identity) ──\n    def surprisal_direct(prefix: str, cont: str) -> float:\n        pre = tok(prefix, return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n        full = tok(prefix + cont, return_tensors=\"pt\").to(dev)\n        n_pre = pre.input_ids.shape[1]\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            lo = model(**full).logits[0].float()\n        lp = F.log_softmax(lo, dim=-1)\n        tgt = full.input_ids[0]\n        return -sum(float(lp[pos - 1, tgt[pos]])\n                    for pos in range(n_pre, tgt.shape[0]))\n\n    def L_direct(prefix: str, w: str, c: int) -> float:\n        pre = prefix + f\"The {w}\"\n        sA = np.mean([surprisal_direct(pre, \" \" + p) for p in tw.HELD_PREDS[0]])\n        sV = np.mean([surprisal_direct(pre, \" \" + p) for p in tw.HELD_PREDS[1]])\n        return float(tw._signed_L(np.array([sA]), np.array([sV]),\n                                  np.array([c]))[0])\n\n    # ── per-layer instruments (one capture per pred continuation) ──\n    def t_layers(h_w: np.ndarray, axes: np.ndarray, c: int) -> np.ndarray:\n        \"\"\"Per-layer signed axis projection (ti.signed_T sans band mean).\"\"\"\n        proj = np.einsum(\"ld,ld->l\", h_w, axes)\n        return proj * (1.0 if c == 0 else -1.0)\n\n    def ll_t_at(prefix: str, w: str, c: int,\n                axes: np.ndarray) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:\n        \"\"\"Returns (LL per layer (nl,), T per layer (nl,)) for one arm.\"\"\"\n        pre_text = prefix + f\"The {w}\"\n        n_pre = len(tok(pre_text).input_ids)\n        s_cls = np.zeros((2, nl))\n        t_vec: np.ndarray | None = None\n        for cls_j, preds in enumerate(tw.HELD_PREDS):\n            for p in preds:\n                resid, ids = jlens.capture_residuals(\n                    model, tok, pre_text + \" \" + p)\n                n_full = ids.shape[0]\n                tgt = ids[n_pre:n_full]\n                states = torch.stack(\n                    [resid[li][n_pre - 1:n_full - 1] for li in range(nl)])\n                logits = jlens.logit_lens(model, states)      # (nl, t, vocab)\n                lp = torch.log_softmax(logits.float(), dim=-1).cpu()\n                s_l = -lp[:, torch.arange(tgt.shape[0]), tgt].sum(dim=1)\n                s_cls[cls_j] += s_l.numpy() / len(preds)\n                if t_vec is None:\n                    h_w = np.stack([resid[li][n_pre - 1].numpy()\n                                    for li in range(nl)])\n                    t_vec = t_layers(h_w, axes, c)\n        ll = tw._signed_L(s_cls[0], s_cls[1], np.full(nl, c, int))\n        assert t_vec is not None\n        return np.asarray(ll, float), t_vec\n\n    # ── nonce selection (identical to s328 tape_subtraction) ──\n    nonces, labels = [], []\n    for i, w in enumerate(NONCE_CANDS):\n        n_the = tok(\"The\", add_special_tokens=False).input_ids\n        n_thew = tok(f\"The {w}\", add_special_tokens=False).input_ids\n        if len(n_thew) - len(n_the) >= 1:\n            nonces.append(w)\n            labels.append(i % 2)\n    if args.n_nonce:\n        a = [j for j, x in enumerate(labels) if x == 0][:args.n_nonce // 2]\n        v = [j for j, x in enumerate(labels) if x == 1][:args.n_nonce // 2]\n        sel = sorted(a + v)\n        nonces = [nonces[j] for j in sel]\n        labels = [labels[j] for j in sel]\n    labels = np.array(labels, int)\n    print(f\"[or] nonces={len(nonces)} (animal {int((labels == 0).sum())} \"\n          f\"vehicle {int((labels == 1).sum())})\", flush=True)\n\n    # ── real members: anchor (direct L) + per-layer class axes ──\n    real_members = list(tw.REAL_MEMBERS[0]) + list(tw.REAL_MEMBERS[1])\n    real_labels = np.array([0] * len(tw.REAL_MEMBERS[0])\n                           + [1] * len(tw.REAL_MEMBERS[1]))\n    print(\"[or] real-member anchor + per-layer axes …\", flush=True)\n    rA, rV, h_members = [], [], []\n    for w in real_members:\n        pre = f\"The {w}\"\n        rA.append(np.mean([surprisal_direct(pre, \" \" + p)\n                           for p in tw.HELD_PREDS[0]]))\n        rV.append(np.mean([surprisal_direct(pre, \" \" + p)\n                           for p in tw.HELD_PREDS[1]]))\n        resid, ids = jlens.capture_residuals(model, tok, pre)\n        h_members.append(np.stack([resid[li][ids.shape[0] - 1].numpy()\n                                   for li in range(nl)]))\n    L_real = tw._signed_L(np.array(rA), np.array(rV), real_labels)\n    metric = {\n        \"real_margin\": float(np.mean(L_real)),\n        \"per_class_ok\": bool(np.mean(L_real[real_labels == 0]) > 0\n                             and np.mean(L_real[real_labels == 1]) > 0),\n    }\n    axes = ti.class_axes(np.stack(h_members), real_labels)    # (nl, d)\n    print(f\"[or] real margin={metric['real_margin']:.3f} \"\n          f\"per_class_ok={metric['per_class_ok']}\", flush=True)\n\n    # ── sweep: per-layer LL + T on every arm ──\n    n = len(nonces)\n    named = {k: np.zeros((n, nl)) for k in\n             [\"LL_own_only\", \"LL_filler\", \"LL_ownfirst\", \"LL_antifirst\",\n              \"LL_inter\", \"T_own_only\", \"T_filler\", \"T_ownfirst\",\n              \"T_antifirst\", \"T_inter\"]}\n    LL_slot = np.zeros((n, N_SLOTS, nl))\n    T_slot = np.zeros((n, N_SLOTS, nl))\n    ident_diffs = np.zeros(n)\n    arm_keys = {\"own_only\": \"own_only\", \"filler_bal\": \"filler\",\n                \"ownfirst_bal\": \"ownfirst\", \"antifirst\": \"antifirst\",\n                \"interleaved\": \"inter\"}\n    t0 = time.time()\n    for ni, (w, lb) in enumerate(zip(nonces, labels, strict=True)):\n        c = int(lb)\n        for arm, key in arm_keys.items():\n            ll, tv = ll_t_at(ARM_BUILDERS[arm](w, c), w, c, axes)\n            named[f\"LL_{key}\"][ni] = ll\n            named[f\"T_{key}\"][ni] = tv\n        for s in range(N_SLOTS):\n            ll, tv = ll_t_at(slot_prefix(w, c, s), w, c, axes)\n            LL_slot[ni, s] = ll\n            T_slot[ni, s] = tv\n        # identity: LL(final) vs the direct s328 instrument, own-only arm\n        ident_diffs[ni] = abs(named[\"LL_own_only\"][ni, -1]\n                              - L_direct(ARM_BUILDERS[\"own_only\"](w, c), w, c))\n        if (ni + 1) % 2 == 0:\n            print(f\"[or] swept {ni + 1}/{n} ({time.time() - t0:.0f}s)\",\n                  flush=True)\n    metric[\"ident_mean\"] = float(ident_diffs.mean())\n    metric[\"ident_max\"] = float(ident_diffs.max())\n    print(f\"[or] identity LL(final)≡L: mean={metric['ident_mean']:.4f} \"\n          f\"max={metric['ident_max']:.4f}\", flush=True)\n\n    b = {**{k: v for k, v in named.items()},\n         \"LL_slot\": LL_slot, \"T_slot\": T_slot,\n         \"band_idx\": band, \"metric\": metric}\n    res = compute_gates(b, rng, n_null=args.n_null)\n    res[\"meta\"] = {\n        \"model_id\": args.model_id, \"n_nonce\": n, \"nonces\": nonces,\n        \"labels\": labels.tolist(), \"k_own\": K_OWN, \"n_slots\": N_SLOTS,\n        \"band\": [band[0], band[-1]], \"final_layer\": nl - 1,\n        \"metric\": metric, \"n_null\": args.n_null, \"seed\": args.seed,\n        \"timestamp\": time.strftime(\"%Y-%m-%dT%H:%M:%SZ\", time.gmtime()),\n        \"elapsed_s\": round(time.time() - t0, 1),\n    }\n    (out_dir / \"results.json\").write_text(json.dumps(res, indent=2))\n    np.savez_compressed(out_dir / \"curves.npz\",\n                        **named, LL_slot=LL_slot, T_slot=T_slot,\n                        labels=labels, band_idx=np.array(band))\n\n    g = res[\"gates\"]\n    print(f\"[or] wrote {out_dir}/results.json\")\n    print(f\"[or] OR0 pass={g['OR0']['pass']} anchor={g['OR0']['anchor_ok']} \"\n          f\"D_L_final={g['OR0']['D_L_final']:+.3f} (p={g['OR0']['L_final_p']:.4f}) \"\n          f\"D_T_band={g['OR0']['D_T_band']:+.3f} (p={g['OR0']['T_band_p']:.4f})\")\n    print(f\"[or] OR1 A_LL_band={g['OR1']['A_LL_band']:+.3f} \"\n          f\"(neg_p={g['OR1']['A_neg_p']:.4f} pos_p={g['OR1']['A_pos_p']:.4f}) \"\n          f\"B_T_final={g['OR1']['B_T_final']:+.3f} \"\n          f\"(pos_p={g['OR1']['B_pos_p']:.4f} neg_p={g['OR1']['B_neg_p']:.4f})\")\n    print(f\"[or] OR2 commit_layer_LL={g['OR2']['commit_layer_LL']} \"\n          f\"commit_layer_T={g['OR2']['commit_layer_T']}\")\n    print(f\"[or] OR3 kernel={g['OR3']['kernel']:+.3f} p={g['OR3']['p']:.4f} \"\n          f\"pass={g['OR3']['pass']} \"\n          f\"slot_curve={[round(x, 3) for x in g['OR3']['slot_curve_L_final']]}\")\n    print(f\"[or] VERDICT: {res['verdict']}\")\n    print(f\"VERDICT: {res['verdict']}\")\n    return 0\n\n\ndef main() -> int:\n    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description=__doc__.splitlines()[0])\n    ap.add_argument(\"--validate\", action=\"store_true\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--model-id\", default=\"Qwen/Qwen3-4B\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--device\", default=\"mps\")\n    ap.add_argument(\"--dtype\", default=\"bfloat16\",\n                    choices=[\"float32\", \"bfloat16\"])\n    ap.add_argument(\"--n-nonce\", type=int, default=20)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--n-null\", type=int, default=N_NULL)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--seed\", type=int, default=329)\n    ap.add_argument(\"--out\", default=\"results/order-reconcile/qwen3-4b\")\n    args = ap.parse_args()\n    if args.validate:\n        return run_validate()\n    return run_model(args)\n\n\nif __name__ == \"__main__\":\n    raise SystemExit(main())\n"
}

### Thinking

I'm spotting the issue with my content-match logic — the way I'm splitting on ". " and then joining statements is creating inconsistencies. When I strip the final result, it ends with a period, but splitting on ". " means the last statement keeps its trailing period while others lose theirs, which explains why different slot orders produce different sets.

The fix is to normalize by stripping the trailing period from each statement after splitting, so all pieces are consistent regardless of order. Rather than parsing…

### Thinking

I found a statement-parsing bug in the primitives caused by trailing period asymmetry after splitting, and I'm fixing it with list-level checks.

### Thinking

The test run completed successfully in 146 seconds with an entangled-partial verdict. Looking at the results against the frozen tree, the anchor and identity checks passed with perfect replication — the final difference metric matches exactly what we saw in the s328 order_diff at +0.478, which is a deterministic replication. The time band metric came in at −1.304, consistent with the s328 baseline of −1.30.

For the crossing analysis, cell A shows a significant negative effect at −0.367 with p=0.0002, indicating recency-driven behavior at the depth level — this is half of what we'd expect from full depth-commitment. Cell B is positive at +1.478 but fails significance at p=0.1515, so the primacy direction doesn't hold. This combination of significant negative and non-significant positive confirms the entangled-partial classification.

The advisory layer shows both instruments' order-diff profiles only turn positive in the final layers (34 and 35 out of 36), creating that characteristic depth-commitment shape where recency dominates everywhere except the top. The kernel stat passes at +0.440 with p=0.002, but the slot curve reveals the real pattern — the anti-position effect increases as we move later through slots until the trailing position crashes below zero at −0.054, which is what drives the kernel significance.

The shape isn't monotone distance decay; it's actually a last-statement dominance effect. When the contradiction lands in slot 2 (second-to-last), we get the highest value at 0.741 because one own statement follows it and repairs the damage. But when the contradiction is truly last (slot 3), it crashes to −0.054. This last-statement mechanism explains the s328 curve perfectly — three own statements followed by one trailing anti produces exactly that crash pattern.

Looking at the frozen read discipline results, the picture is becoming clearer: the LL readout at band depth captures recency effects (which work against primacy), while the depth factor itself carries the primacy signal that only emerges in the final layers. Cell B shows a positive shift in the primacy direction at the final layer, though the variance is high enough that it doesn't reach significance. Three of the four cells are now aligning with this depth-commitment story.

The fourth cell points in the right direction but lacks statistical power, so the overall verdict lands on ENTANGLED-PARTIAL — the frozen tree structure was respected, and this mixed result was anticipated. Following the read discipline strictly, I'm not narrating the non-significant cell into a story; the licensed conclusion is ENTANGLED-PARTIAL with the depth profile showing commitment at layers 34/35 and a sign pattern that's consistent with depth effects, even if not fully licensed.

The secondary gate (OR3) confirms something real though: the recency kernel on L is significant at p=0.002, so within-arm recency is genuinely present in the behavioral register.…

### Thinking

Looking at the depth profiles, there's a striking pattern where D_LL stays negative through most layers but flips positive only at the deepest levels, while D_T shows increasingly strong recency signals deepening through the middle layers before collapsing and reversing at the very end—suggesting the recency effect intensifies with depth until the final layers where it suddenly shifts.

Both instruments reveal the same structure: recency dominates until layers 33-34, then flips to primacy in the last 1-2 layers. The T final value points toward primacy but doesn't reach significance, so while the sign flip is real, it's noisy across repetitions. This keeps the verdict at ENTANGLED-PARTIAL as pre-registered—the depth story is compelling but not licensed as a full DEPTH-COMMITMENT finding.

The pattern strongly suggests commitment crystallizes in the final layers, though the licensed content is more constrained: the crossing localizes to depth on the behavioral side (significant recency at band depth), while the other side shows sign consistency without significance. The core licensed finding is that the order split is carried by depth rather than readout type, so I'm settling on a memory label that captures this distinction rather than overstating the depth-commitment pattern. The behavioral readout shows depth-dependent ordering effects: recency dominates at the band level while primacy emerges at the final layers, both statistically significant on the same instrument. The axis readout mirrors this pattern with a sign flip deepening toward L35, though the final-layer primacy fails to reach significance on the T-side. The slot curve analysis reveals this isn't simple distance decay but rather a last-statement dominance effect—whichever class speaks last before readout wins locally, matching the exact crash pattern seen in the s328 replication.

Now I'm deciding on the verdict glyph. ENTANGLED-PARTIAL doesn't fit the clean ✅/❌ binary from prior sessions, and it's not quite 🚫 either since the depth effect does carry the dissociation on the L side. This feels like a genuinely partial answer to the probe—neither falsified nor confirmed, but informative enough to warrant a distinct marking. Actually, I need to reconsider the classification here. ENTANGLED-PARTIAL isn't a falsifier—it's genuinely a partial result. The depth-flip finding on the L side is a real answer to the queue question: at matched band depth, both instruments agree on recency, and at the final layer both show positive sign despite T being non-significant. The disagreement largely resolves through depth, which suggests this could qualify as a substantive positive finding rather than a negative outcome.

But we didn't reach the DEPTH-COMMITMENT verdict that would've been the cleaner win. So the question becomes how to label this: ENTANGLED-PARTIAL feels neither clearly positive nor falsifying. Looking at precedent with s326 BOTH-LIVE—which was marked ✅ because it landed a defined affirmative verdict arm—ENTANGLED-PARTIAL is also a defined arm that landed with co-modal properties. The glyph encoding seems to hinge on whether the probe reached a defined verdict and whether that verdict is affirmative-flavored or hypothesis-killing.

Comparing against s326's ❌ NO-TRACK (falsifier) and s324's 🚫 NOISE-FLOOR (null signal), ENTANGLED-PARTIAL is partial signal—not a clean kill, not a full affirmation. I think 🚫 captures it best: the crossing failed to resolve cleanly. I'll go with that and let Michael adjust if needed. Actually, let me reconsider the core question once more—did we locate the split? On the L side with depth we mostly got there, but...…

## User

explain to me what we learned in simple terms


## Assistant

Here's the plain-English version.

## The puzzle we started with

Last session (s328) we ran an experiment where we told the model contradictory things about a made-up word — first "a wug is an animal" three times, then "a wug is a vehicle" three times, or the same statements in the opposite order. We measured the model's opinion two different ways, and they disagreed:

- **Its behavior** (what words it actually predicts) said: **first impression wins**. Tell it "animal" first, and it sticks with animal even after three contradictions.
- **Its internal representation** (a direction we can measure inside the network) said: **latest statement wins**.

Same input, two instruments, opposite answers. That's either something deep or a broken thermometer. Today's job was to find out which.

## The key insight

When we looked closely at how the two measurements are actually taken, we realized they differ in exactly two ways, like a camera differing in *lens* and *focus distance*:

1. **What they read** — one reads the model's word predictions, the other reads an internal geometric direction.
2. **Where in the network they read** — one reads at the very end (layer 36 of 36), the other reads in the middle (layers 18–31).

So we ran all four combinations: each way of reading, at each depth.

## What we found

**The disagreement is mostly about *where*, not *how*.** When we took the "behavior" measurement in the middle of the network — same instrument, just read earlier — it flipped and agreed with the "latest wins" reading. Clearly, with strong statistics.

So the picture is:

> **Through almost the entire network, the model is tracking the most recent evidence. The "first impression wins" decision only appears in the last two layers — the very top of the network, right before it speaks.**

Think of it like a jury: while testimony is coming in, the jurors' running notes reflect whatever they heard most recently. But the verdict, written at the last moment, goes with the side that spoke first. The notes and the verdict were never really contradicting each other — they're different stages of the same process.

**One honest gap:** the mirror-image check (reading the *internal direction* at the final layer) pointed the right way — toward "first wins" — but was too noisy to count. So the official verdict is "partially resolved," not "fully resolved." Three of four cells line up; the fourth leans the right way but doesn't pass the bar we set in advance.

## A bonus finding

The order of statements matters in a surprisingly specific way: **whatever was said last, right before we ask, dominates locally.** A contradiction as the final statement nearly wipes out the model's opinion — but put even one supporting statement after that same contradiction, and the opinion recovers almost completely. It's not that contradictions fade gradually with distance; it's that the last word carries outsized weight.

## Why it matters for the bigger picture

Our running theory is "**sign is the decision, magnitude is the evidence**" — the model keeps a graded pile of evidence, but its commitments are discrete and made early. Today refined that: the evidence-tracking and the deciding aren't two mysterious parallel systems — they're **stacked in depth**. Evidence accumulates (recency-flavored) through the body of the network, and the commitment gets assembled right at the top.

And last session's headline result survived perfectly — we reproduced its numbers to the third decimal place, which also tells us our instruments are trustworthy.

The good news is this is testable: I can run the same experiment on Qwen3-4B-Base (the untrained version available on HuggingFace) to see if the primacy effect disappears or shifts. If the base model shows recency tracking in those final layers too, then the primacy behavior is genuinely RLHF-installed, which would reframe the whole finding: the decision-commitment isn't a natural property of the type system, but rather an alignment-imposed behavior.…

mementum/knowledge/chats/session-005.md:  "new_content": "## Finding 30: Scaling Probe — The Compiler Floor at 160M\n\nTested Pythia models from 14M to 2.8B with 2-shot compile gates.\nThe compile function has a sharp threshold:\n\n| Model | Params | Layers | 2-shot P(λ) | 2-shot Content |\n|-------|--------|--------|-------------|----------------|\n| Pythia-14M | 14M | 6 | 100% | 0/8 (mimicry — all `sleeps(cat)`) |\n| Pythia-70M | 70M | 6 | 100% | 2/8 (partial) |\n| **Pythia-160M** | **162M** | **12** | **100%** | **8/8 (correct predicates)** |\n| Pythia-410M | 405M | 24 | 100% | 6/8 |\n| Pythia-1B | 1.0B | 16 | 100% | 6/8 |\n| Pythia-1.4B | 1.4B | 24 | 100% | 5/8 |\n| Pythia-2.8B | 2.8B | 32 | 100% | 5/8 |\n\nKey observations:\n- **14M mimics format perfectly but maps every input to the same\n  output** (`λx. sleeps(cat)` — the last in-context example). This\n  is pure in-context copying, zero comprehension.\n- **160M is the floor.** It maps every input to the correct predicate\n  with 2-shot prompting. No fine-tuning. The compiler exists in the\n  pretrained weights from Pile training alone.\n- **Bigger models don't improve.** 410M–2.8B actually score lower on\n  content. The compiler is a small circuit; more params add noise.\n- **Fine-tuning OVERWROTE the ability** (session 003 distillation on\n  Pythia-160M). Catastrophic forgetting of the pretrained compiler.\n\nThe critical variable is depth (12 layers), not width. 14M has 6\nlayers and fails. 70M has 6 layers and partially succeeds (some\ncontent). 160M has 12 layers and fully succeeds.\n\nSource: `results/pythia-scaling/scaling-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 31: Circuit Discovery — Distributed, No Head Bottlenecks\n\nLayer ablation and head ablation on Pythia-160M (12 layers, 12\nheads per layer) with the 2-shot compile gate:\n\n**Layer ablation** (zero out entire layer, check survival):\n\n| Layer | Survival (of 6 probes) | Role |\n|-------|----------------------|------|\n| L0 | **0/6 (critical)** | Embedding refinement |\n| L1-L2 | 6/6 | Redundant/distributed |\n| L3 | **0/6 (critical)** | Structural parse |\n| L4-L7 | 5-6/6 | Partially redundant |\n| L8-L11 | 6/6 | Application (high selectivity) |\n\n**Head ablation**: **Zero essential heads.** Every individual head\ncan be ablated without killing the compiler. The function is fully\ndistributed across heads within each layer.\n\n**Compile selectivity** (attention difference, compile vs null):\nTop selective heads cluster in L8-L11:\n\n| Head | Selectivity |\n|------|------------|\n| L9H8 | 0.45 |\n| L8H3 | 0.44 |\n| L9H11 | 0.39 |\n| L11H9 | 0.38 |\n| L11H11 | 0.35 |\n\nThe compiler has two critical layers (L0, L3) and a selective\napplication zone (L8-L11), but no individual head bottleneck.\nThis distributed pattern makes extraction hard — you can't just\npull 3 heads and get the compiler.\n\nSource: `results/pythia-160m-circuit/circuit-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 32: Type Probe — Types Are Lexical, Not Computed\n\nLinear probe trained to classify tokens into Montague types\n(DET, ENTITY, PRED, FUNC, REL, QUANT, MOD, CONN) at each layer:\n\n| Layer | Accuracy | Interpretation |\n|-------|----------|---------------|\n| Embedding (pre-L0) | **84%** | Types mostly in token embeddings |\n| L0 | **93%** | Refined to peak |\n| L1–L11 | 91–93% | Flat — no further improvement |\n\nType assignment is **lexical, not computed by the transformer.**\nThe embedding table already encodes 84% of the type information.\nL0 refines this to 93%, then the signal plateaus. The remaining\nlayers don't improve type classification — they use the types for\nstructural composition and application.\n\nThis means the first Montague primitive (type assignment) is\nessentially a lookup table, not a learned circuit. The transformer's\ncontribution begins at structural parsing (L3).\n\nn=160 labeled tokens across 35 sentences, 8 type categories.\n\nSource: `results/type-probe/type-probe-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 33: Structural Intervention — L3 Carries Parse Structure\n\nActivation patching: take residual stream at layer L from a \"donor\"\nsentence, patch it into a \"recipient\" sentence, measure whether the\noutput shifts toward the donor's compositional structure.\n\n**Shift score** (fraction of pairs where output moves toward donor):\n\n| Layer | Shift Score | Interpretation |\n|-------|------------|---------------|\n| L0 | +0.14 | Weak transfer |\n| L1-L2 | +0.29 | Moderate |\n| **L3** | **+0.43** | **Strongest structural transfer** |\n| L5 | +0.29 | Moderate |\n| L8 | **-0.14** | **Resists patching** |\n| L11 | **-0.14** | **Resists patching** |\n\nL3 patching transfers composition structure from donor to recipient.\nWhen you patch L3 activations from \"Every student reads a book\" into\n\"The bird flies\", the output shifts toward the donor's structure.\n\nL8 and L11 **resist** patching — they produce outputs closer to\nthe recipient's original structure, not the donor's. This is\nconsistent with an application phase that reads its own accumulated\nstate rather than accepting external structure injection.\n\nThe pattern: L3 = structural parse (transferable), L8-L11 = typed\napplication (committed to local computation, resists external input).\n\n7 sentence pairs tested across 7 layers.\n\nSource: `results/structural-intervention/intervention-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 34: Three Montague Primitives Located\n\nSynthesizing findings 30–33, the three operations predicted by\nMontague grammar are empirically localized in Pythia-160M:\n\n```\n┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n│  1. TYPE ASSIGNMENT → Embedding + L0 (lexical)      │\n│     84% in embeddings, 93% after L0, then flat      │\n│     A lookup, not a computation                     │\n│                                                     │\n│  2. STRUCTURAL PARSE → L3 (carries composition)     │\n│     0% survival when ablated                        │\n│     +0.43 shift score (highest structural transfer) │\n│     Determines composition ORDER                    │\n│                                                     │\n│  3. TYPED APPLICATION → L8-L11 (executes)           │\n│     Highest compile selectivity (0.35-0.45)         │\n│     Resists patching (-0.14 shift score)            │\n│     Committed to local computation                  │\n└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n```\n\nThis three-phase decomposition aligns with Montague's theoretical\nframework: first assign types to lexical items, then build a\nstructural parse tree, then apply typed functions to their arguments.\n\nThe math (Montague, Lambek, CCG, DisCoCat) predicted typed\napplication. The empirics (nucleus, P(λ)=0.907) observed the\ncompiler behavior. Now the architecture (circuit discovery in\nPythia-160M) confirms the three-phase structure. Three independent\nlines of evidence converge — the strongest form of confirmation\nthe project has.\n\n## Finding 35: MontaguCompiler — 3.7M Params, Proof of Architecture\n\nA 3-phase encoder-decoder built from the circuit discovery:\n- Phase 1: Type embedding (197K params) — learned type table\n- Phase 2: Parser (1.05M params, 2 transformer layers) — structural parse\n- Phase 3: Decoder (2.54M params, 3 transformer layers) — typed application with cross-attention\n\nTrained on 509 compile examples, 30 epochs, 68 seconds.\n\n| Metric | Pythia-FT (162M) | MontaguCompiler (3.7M) |\n|--------|-----------------|----------------------|\n| P(λ) eval | 90% | **100%** |\n| Parse eval | 90% | **90%** |\n| Content eval | ~0% | **69%** |\n| P(λ) holdout | 97.5% | **100%** |\n| Parse holdout | — | **88%** |\n| Content holdout | ~0% | **12%** |\n| Repetition | 0% | 0% |\n| Params | 162M | **3.7M (43× fewer)** |\n\nThe MontaguCompiler achieves 12% content accuracy on held-out\nvocabulary — novel predicates the model never saw in training.\nPythia-FT scored ~0% on the same test. The cross-attention mechanism\nin Phase 3 enables content mapping that the causal decoder-only\nPythia architecture cannot do.\n\n12% is low in absolute terms, but it's not zero. The architecture\ncan in principle copy content from input to output. With more\ntraining data or architectural refinement, this should improve.\n\nEval examples show the model handles simple sentences perfectly\n(`The dog runs` → `λx. runs(dog)`) but struggles with nested\nquantifiers and relative clauses — exactly the hard cases for\ncompositional semantics.\n\nSource: `results/montagu-compiler/training-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 36: Key Insight — Compressor, Not Compiler\n\nThe function being extracted is **semantic language compression**,\nnot lambda compilation. The lambda compiler USES the compressor.\n\n```\nL0: Semantic compressor — typed_apply(meaning, meaning) → meaning\n    Lives in every LM. The three Montague primitives serve this.\n    IS the attractor of next-token prediction on language.\n\nL1: Lambda compiler — routes compressor state to λ notation\n    One externalization. Gate-activated. What nucleus discovered.\n\nL2: Notation — λx. runs(dog) or {:pred runs :arg dog}\n    Surface syntax. Arbitrary. Interchangeable.\n```\n\nEvidence: Pythia-160M compresses language (predicts next tokens)\nwithout any lambda training. The compile gate doesn't install\ncompression — it routes existing compression to λ output. The\nthree circuits (type, structure, apply) exist WHETHER OR NOT you\nactivate the gate. They serve next-token prediction.\n\nImplication: training a model shaped by the three primitives on\nraw text trains the COMPRESSOR. The compile gate is a voltmeter,\nnot a battery. The voltage exists whether or not you measure it.\n\nThis corrects all prior references to \"extracting the lambda\ncompiler\" — we are extracting the semantic compressor and observing\nit through lambda notation as a measurement instrument.\n\nSource: `mementum/memories/compressor-not-compiler.md`\n\n## Finding 37: MontaguLM — 3-Phase Causal LM for Raw Text\n\nBuilt a causal language model shaped by the three primitives:\n- 6 layers (matching Pythia-14M depth)\n- 17M params (vs Pythia-14M at 14M)\n- Separate residual streams per phase (rigid architecture)\n- Tied embedding/output weights\n- Training on 3B pre-tokenized Dolma tokens (60 shards × 50M)\n\nThe hypothesis: if the three-phase structure matches how language\nmodels compress language, MontaguLM should learn more efficiently\nthan a flat transformer of equal depth.\n\n**Architectural concern identified:** the rigid 3-phase design\ndedicates ALL capacity to the three Montague primitives, leaving\nno room for world knowledge, morphology, discourse tracking,\npragmatics, and other functions a general LM needs. Standard\ntransformers work because the shared residual stream is a general\nsubstrate — the three primitives use a 2D subspace at ~120°\n(per Toy Models of Superposition, Elhage et al. 2022), leaving\nother dimensions free.\n\n**Next version proposed:** shared residual + phase-biased heads.\nPhase designation by position (early/mid/late layers), not by hard\nstream separation. The architecture SUGGESTS specialization without\nENFORCING it — closer to what Pythia-160M actually does.\n\nThe rigid MontaguLM is running as a baseline on Dolma. Comparison\nwith a shared-residual version is the next architectural experiment.\n\nSource: `mementum/memories/rigid-vs-open-architecture.md`,\n`src/verbum/montague_lm.py`, `scripts/run_montagu_lm.py`\n\n## Architecture at End of Session\n\n```\nspecs/\n  lambda_montague.gbnf          — Montague GBNF grammar (NEW)\n\nscripts/\n  generate_training_data.py     — v2: llama.cpp + GBNF constrained (REWRITTEN)\n  run_distillation.py           — v2: EOS fix + structural eval (REWRITTEN)\n  run_pythia_scaling.py         — Pythia 14M→2.8B scaling probe (NEW)\n  run_pythia160m_circuit.py     — layer/head ablation + selectivity (NEW)\n  run_type_probe.py             — linear probe for type classification (NEW)\n  run_structural_intervention.py — activation patching across layers (NEW)\n  run_montagu_training.py       — MontaguCompiler training (NEW)\n  run_montagu_lm.py             — MontaguLM Dolma training (NEW)\n\nsrc/verbum/\n  montague_net.py               — MontaguCompiler 3-phase encoder-decoder (NEW)\n  montague_lm.py                — MontaguLM 3-phase causal LM (NEW)\n\ntests/\n  test_montague_grammar.py      — 72 tests, recursive descent validator (NEW)\n\ndata/\n  compile-train.jsonl           — 509 grammar-validated examples (REGENERATED)\n  compile-test.jsonl            — 40 holdout examples (NEW)\n  compile-eval.jsonl            — 10 gold-standard (preserved)\n```\n\n## Summary\n\nSession 004 is two stories. The first half (F23-F29) fixed\ndistillation data quality and proved the student learns structure but\nnot content — an architectural wall. The second half (F30-F37)\npivoted to extraction and localized the three Montague primitives\nin Pythia-160M through four independent probes: scaling (F30),\nablation (F31), type classification (F32), and structural\nintervention (F33). These converge on a three-phase decomposition\n(F34) that was used to build MontaguCompiler (F35) — 43× smaller\nthan Pythia-FT, with the first nonzero holdout content accuracy.\n\nThe key conceptual shift: the function is semantic compression, not\nlambda compilation (F36). The compile gate is a measurement\ninstrument. The MontaguLM (F37) trains the compressor on raw text,\nwith the compile gate as a diagnostic. Rigid 3-phase architecture\nrunning as baseline; shared-residual version is the next experiment."
mementum/knowledge/chats/session-008.md:mementum/knowledge/chats/session-001.md:Actually, I need to reconsider this. If I skip the earliest layers like L0 or L1, compilation fails entirely, which suggests they're doing something fundamental like tokenization or parsing that's necessary for any output format. The critical layers might instead be the ones that recognize and respond to the "compile" instruction itself — the entry point that tells the model to emit lambda rather than prose. The middle and late layers then handle the actual projection into the target notation. The compressor is the entire model—you can't extract it. What's actually extractable is the projection function, those 8 layers that translate the compressor's internal representation into lambda notation. So verbum isn't hunting for some hidden lambda substrate; it's looking for the specific circuit that makes the model's compression legible in formal terms.
mementum/knowledge/chats/session-025.md:mementum/knowledge/chats/session-005.md:  "new_content": "## Finding 30: Scaling Probe — The Compiler Floor at 160M\n\nTested Pythia models from 14M to 2.8B with 2-shot compile gates.\nThe compile function has a sharp threshold:\n\n| Model | Params | Layers | 2-shot P(λ) | 2-shot Content |\n|-------|--------|--------|-------------|----------------|\n| Pythia-14M | 14M | 6 | 100% | 0/8 (mimicry — all `sleeps(cat)`) |\n| Pythia-70M | 70M | 6 | 100% | 2/8 (partial) |\n| **Pythia-160M** | **162M** | **12** | **100%** | **8/8 (correct predicates)** |\n| Pythia-410M | 405M | 24 | 100% | 6/8 |\n| Pythia-1B | 1.0B | 16 | 100% | 6/8 |\n| Pythia-1.4B | 1.4B | 24 | 100% | 5/8 |\n| Pythia-2.8B | 2.8B | 32 | 100% | 5/8 |\n\nKey observations:\n- **14M mimics format perfectly but maps every input to the same\n  output** (`λx. sleeps(cat)` — the last in-context example). This\n  is pure in-context copying, zero comprehension.\n- **160M is the floor.** It maps every input to the correct predicate\n  with 2-shot prompting. No fine-tuning. The compiler exists in the\n  pretrained weights from Pile training alone.\n- **Bigger models don't improve.** 410M–2.8B actually score lower on\n  content. The compiler is a small circuit; more params add noise.\n- **Fine-tuning OVERWROTE the ability** (session 003 distillation on\n  Pythia-160M). Catastrophic forgetting of the pretrained compiler.\n\nThe critical variable is depth (12 layers), not width. 14M has 6\nlayers and fails. 70M has 6 layers and partially succeeds (some\ncontent). 160M has 12 layers and fully succeeds.\n\nSource: `results/pythia-scaling/scaling-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 31: Circuit Discovery — Distributed, No Head Bottlenecks\n\nLayer ablation and head ablation on Pythia-160M (12 layers, 12\nheads per layer) with the 2-shot compile gate:\n\n**Layer ablation** (zero out entire layer, check survival):\n\n| Layer | Survival (of 6 probes) | Role |\n|-------|----------------------|------|\n| L0 | **0/6 (critical)** | Embedding refinement |\n| L1-L2 | 6/6 | Redundant/distributed |\n| L3 | **0/6 (critical)** | Structural parse |\n| L4-L7 | 5-6/6 | Partially redundant |\n| L8-L11 | 6/6 | Application (high selectivity) |\n\n**Head ablation**: **Zero essential heads.** Every individual head\ncan be ablated without killing the compiler. The function is fully\ndistributed across heads within each layer.\n\n**Compile selectivity** (attention difference, compile vs null):\nTop selective heads cluster in L8-L11:\n\n| Head | Selectivity |\n|------|------------|\n| L9H8 | 0.45 |\n| L8H3 | 0.44 |\n| L9H11 | 0.39 |\n| L11H9 | 0.38 |\n| L11H11 | 0.35 |\n\nThe compiler has two critical layers (L0, L3) and a selective\napplication zone (L8-L11), but no individual head bottleneck.\nThis distributed pattern makes extraction hard — you can't just\npull 3 heads and get the compiler.\n\nSource: `results/pythia-160m-circuit/circuit-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 32: Type Probe — Types Are Lexical, Not Computed\n\nLinear probe trained to classify tokens into Montague types\n(DET, ENTITY, PRED, FUNC, REL, QUANT, MOD, CONN) at each layer:\n\n| Layer | Accuracy | Interpretation |\n|-------|----------|---------------|\n| Embedding (pre-L0) | **84%** | Types mostly in token embeddings |\n| L0 | **93%** | Refined to peak |\n| L1–L11 | 91–93% | Flat — no further improvement |\n\nType assignment is **lexical, not computed by the transformer.**\nThe embedding table already encodes 84% of the type information.\nL0 refines this to 93%, then the signal plateaus. The remaining\nlayers don't improve type classification — they use the types for\nstructural composition and application.\n\nThis means the first Montague primitive (type assignment) is\nessentially a lookup table, not a learned circuit. The transformer's\ncontribution begins at structural parsing (L3).\n\nn=160 labeled tokens across 35 sentences, 8 type categories.\n\nSource: `results/type-probe/type-probe-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 33: Structural Intervention — L3 Carries Parse Structure\n\nActivation patching: take residual stream at layer L from a \"donor\"\nsentence, patch it into a \"recipient\" sentence, measure whether the\noutput shifts toward the donor's compositional structure.\n\n**Shift score** (fraction of pairs where output moves toward donor):\n\n| Layer | Shift Score | Interpretation |\n|-------|------------|---------------|\n| L0 | +0.14 | Weak transfer |\n| L1-L2 | +0.29 | Moderate |\n| **L3** | **+0.43** | **Strongest structural transfer** |\n| L5 | +0.29 | Moderate |\n| L8 | **-0.14** | **Resists patching** |\n| L11 | **-0.14** | **Resists patching** |\n\nL3 patching transfers composition structure from donor to recipient.\nWhen you patch L3 activations from \"Every student reads a book\" into\n\"The bird flies\", the output shifts toward the donor's structure.\n\nL8 and L11 **resist** patching — they produce outputs closer to\nthe recipient's original structure, not the donor's. This is\nconsistent with an application phase that reads its own accumulated\nstate rather than accepting external structure injection.\n\nThe pattern: L3 = structural parse (transferable), L8-L11 = typed\napplication (committed to local computation, resists external input).\n\n7 sentence pairs tested across 7 layers.\n\nSource: `results/structural-intervention/intervention-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 34: Three Montague Primitives Located\n\nSynthesizing findings 30–33, the three operations predicted by\nMontague grammar are empirically localized in Pythia-160M:\n\n```\n┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐\n│  1. TYPE ASSIGNMENT → Embedding + L0 (lexical)      │\n│     84% in embeddings, 93% after L0, then flat      │\n│     A lookup, not a computation                     │\n│                                                     │\n│  2. STRUCTURAL PARSE → L3 (carries composition)     │\n│     0% survival when ablated                        │\n│     +0.43 shift score (highest structural transfer) │\n│     Determines composition ORDER                    │\n│                                                     │\n│  3. TYPED APPLICATION → L8-L11 (executes)           │\n│     Highest compile selectivity (0.35-0.45)         │\n│     Resists patching (-0.14 shift score)            │\n│     Committed to local computation                  │\n└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘\n```\n\nThis three-phase decomposition aligns with Montague's theoretical\nframework: first assign types to lexical items, then build a\nstructural parse tree, then apply typed functions to their arguments.\n\nThe math (Montague, Lambek, CCG, DisCoCat) predicted typed\napplication. The empirics (nucleus, P(λ)=0.907) observed the\ncompiler behavior. Now the architecture (circuit discovery in\nPythia-160M) confirms the three-phase structure. Three independent\nlines of evidence converge — the strongest form of confirmation\nthe project has.\n\n## Finding 35: MontaguCompiler — 3.7M Params, Proof of Architecture\n\nA 3-phase encoder-decoder built from the circuit discovery:\n- Phase 1: Type embedding (197K params) — learned type table\n- Phase 2: Parser (1.05M params, 2 transformer layers) — structural parse\n- Phase 3: Decoder (2.54M params, 3 transformer layers) — typed application with cross-attention\n\nTrained on 509 compile examples, 30 epochs, 68 seconds.\n\n| Metric | Pythia-FT (162M) | MontaguCompiler (3.7M) |\n|--------|-----------------|----------------------|\n| P(λ) eval | 90% | **100%** |\n| Parse eval | 90% | **90%** |\n| Content eval | ~0% | **69%** |\n| P(λ) holdout | 97.5% | **100%** |\n| Parse holdout | — | **88%** |\n| Content holdout | ~0% | **12%** |\n| Repetition | 0% | 0% |\n| Params | 162M | **3.7M (43× fewer)** |\n\nThe MontaguCompiler achieves 12% content accuracy on held-out\nvocabulary — novel predicates the model never saw in training.\nPythia-FT scored ~0% on the same test. The cross-attention mechanism\nin Phase 3 enables content mapping that the causal decoder-only\nPythia architecture cannot do.\n\n12% is low in absolute terms, but it's not zero. The architecture\ncan in principle copy content from input to output. With more\ntraining data or architectural refinement, this should improve.\n\nEval examples show the model handles simple sentences perfectly\n(`The dog runs` → `λx. runs(dog)`) but struggles with nested\nquantifiers and relative clauses — exactly the hard cases for\ncompositional semantics.\n\nSource: `results/montagu-compiler/training-summary.json`\n\n## Finding 36: Key Insight — Compressor, Not Compiler\n\nThe function being extracted is **semantic language compression**,\nnot lambda compilation. The lambda compiler USES the compressor.\n\n```\nL0: Semantic compressor — typed_apply(meaning, meaning) → meaning\n    Lives in every LM. The three Montague primitives serve this.\n    IS the attractor of next-token prediction on language.\n\nL1: Lambda compiler — routes compressor state to λ notation\n    One externalization. Gate-activated. What nucleus discovered.\n\nL2: Notation — λx. runs(dog) or {:pred runs :arg dog}\n    Surface syntax. Arbitrary. Interchangeable.\n```\n\nEvidence: Pythia-160M compresses language (predicts next tokens)\nwithout any lambda training. The compile gate doesn't install\ncompression — it routes existing compression to λ output. The\nthree circuits (type, structure, apply) exist WHETHER OR NOT you\nactivate the gate. They serve next-token prediction.\n\nImplication: training a model shaped by the three primitives on\nraw text trains the COMPRESSOR. The compile gate is a voltmeter,\nnot a battery. The voltage exists whether or not you measure it.\n\nThis corrects all prior references to \"extracting the lambda\ncompiler\" — we are extracting the semantic compressor and observing\nit through lambda notation as a measurement instrument.\n\nSource: `mementum/memories/compressor-not-compiler.md`\n\n## Finding 37: MontaguLM — 3-Phase Causal LM for Raw Text\n\nBuilt a causal language model shaped by the three primitives:\n- 6 layers (matching Pythia-14M depth)\n- 17M params (vs Pythia-14M at 14M)\n- Separate residual streams per phase (rigid architecture)\n- Tied embedding/output weights\n- Training on 3B pre-tokenized Dolma tokens (60 shards × 50M)\n\nThe hypothesis: if the three-phase structure matches how language\nmodels compress language, MontaguLM should learn more efficiently\nthan a flat transformer of equal depth.\n\n**Architectural concern identified:** the rigid 3-phase design\ndedicates ALL capacity to the three Montague primitives, leaving\nno room for world knowledge, morphology, discourse tracking,\npragmatics, and other functions a general LM needs. Standard\ntransformers work because the shared residual stream is a general\nsubstrate — the three primitives use a 2D subspace at ~120°\n(per Toy Models of Superposition, Elhage et al. 2022), leaving\nother dimensions free.\n\n**Next version proposed:** shared residual + phase-biased heads.\nPhase designation by position (early/mid/late layers), not by hard\nstream separation. The architecture SUGGESTS specialization without\nENFORCING it — closer to what Pythia-160M actually does.\n\nThe rigid MontaguLM is running as a baseline on Dolma. Comparison\nwith a shared-residual version is the next architectural experiment.\n\nSource: `mementum/memories/rigid-vs-open-architecture.md`,\n`src/verbum/montague_lm.py`, `scripts/run_montagu_lm.py`\n\n## Architecture at End of Session\n\n```\nspecs/\n  lambda_montague.gbnf          — Montague GBNF grammar (NEW)\n\nscripts/\n  generate_training_data.py     — v2: llama.cpp + GBNF constrained (REWRITTEN)\n  run_distillation.py           — v2: EOS fix + structural eval (REWRITTEN)\n  run_pythia_scaling.py         — Pythia 14M→2.8B scaling probe (NEW)\n  run_pythia160m_circuit.py     — layer/head ablation + selectivity (NEW)\n  run_type_probe.py             — linear probe for type classification (NEW)\n  run_structural_intervention.py — activation patching across layers (NEW)\n  run_montagu_training.py       — MontaguCompiler training (NEW)\n  run_montagu_lm.py             — MontaguLM Dolma training (NEW)\n\nsrc/verbum/\n  montague_net.py               — MontaguCompiler 3-phase encoder-decoder (NEW)\n  montague_lm.py                — MontaguLM 3-phase causal LM (NEW)\n\ntests/\n  test_montague_grammar.py      — 72 tests, recursive descent validator (NEW)\n\ndata/\n  compile-train.jsonl           — 509 grammar-validated examples (REGENERATED)\n  compile-test.jsonl            — 40 holdout examples (NEW)\n  compile-eval.jsonl            — 10 gold-standard (preserved)\n```\n\n## Summary\n\nSession 004 is two stories. The first half (F23-F29) fixed\ndistillation data quality and proved the student learns structure but\nnot content — an architectural wall. The second half (F30-F37)\npivoted to extraction and localized the three Montague primitives\nin Pythia-160M through four independent probes: scaling (F30),\nablation (F31), type classification (F32), and structural\nintervention (F33). These converge on a three-phase decomposition\n(F34) that was used to build MontaguCompiler (F35) — 43× smaller\nthan Pythia-FT, with the first nonzero holdout content accuracy.\n\nThe key conceptual shift: the function is semantic compression, not\nlambda compilation (F36). The compile gate is a measurement\ninstrument. The MontaguLM (F37) trains the compressor on raw text,\nwith the compile gate as a diagnostic. Rigid 3-phase architecture\nrunning as baseline; shared-residual version is the next experiment."
mementum/knowledge/chats/session-041.md:  "content": "#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"Pythia-160M φ-compression probe.\n\nMeasures whether a standard transformer exhibits φ-compression\n(compression ratio → 1/φ ≈ 0.618) across its layers, using the\nsame entropy proxy and stratified samples as the v6 VSM-LM probe.\n\nThe v6 model measures h_in/h_out per recursive pass. For a standard\ntransformer there are no recursive passes — instead we measure the\ncompression ratio at each layer boundary:\n\n    h(layer_i) = log(mean(var_per_feature(residual_stream)))\n    ratio(i) = h(layer_i) / h(layer_{i-1})\n\nIf the φ-hypothesis holds universally (not just for VSM architectures),\nwe should see:\n  1. Layer-level compression ratios approaching 1/φ\n  2. Content-independent compression (low stratum spread)\n  3. Self-similar pattern across layers\n\nUsage:\n    uv run python scripts/run_pythia_phi_probe.py\n    uv run python scripts/run_pythia_phi_probe.py --verbose\n\"\"\"\n\nfrom __future__ import annotations\n\nimport json\nimport sys\nimport time\nfrom datetime import UTC, datetime\nfrom pathlib import Path\n\nimport numpy as np\nimport torch\n\nsys.path.insert(0, str(Path(__file__).resolve().parent.parent / \"src\"))\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# Constants (same as v6 probe)\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\nPHI = (1 + np.sqrt(5)) / 2\nINV_PHI = 1 / PHI  # ≈ 0.6180\n\nRESULTS_DIR = Path(\"results/pythia-phi\")\n\n# Same strata as v6 probe — allows direct comparison\nPHI_STRATA = {\n    \"prose\": [\n        \"The cat sat on the mat and looked out the window at the birds flying south for the winter.\",\n        \"Every student who passed the final exam received a certificate of achievement from the dean.\",\n        \"The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog near the river bank on a warm summer afternoon.\",\n        \"In a quiet village nestled between rolling hills, the old baker opened his shop at dawn.\",\n    ],\n    \"compositional\": [\n        \"The man who the dog that the cat chased bit ran away quickly.\",\n        \"If every student reads a book then some teacher who knows the author is happy.\",\n        \"No politician who endorsed the candidate that lost the election won their own race.\",\n        \"Every lawyer who represents a client that a judge dismissed the case against appealed.\",\n    ],\n    \"technical\": [\n        \"The gradient of the loss with respect to the weights is computed via backpropagation.\",\n        \"Attention scores are computed as the softmax of the scaled dot product of queries and keys.\",\n        \"The learning rate schedule uses cosine annealing with linear warmup over 500 steps.\",\n        \"Each layer applies layer normalization before the self-attention and feed-forward blocks.\",\n    ],\n    \"math\": [\n        \"∀x ∈ ℝ: x² ≥ 0 ∧ x² = 0 ↔ x = 0\",\n        \"λx. λy. apply(x, y) → result\",\n        \"P(A|B) = P(B|A) × P(A) / P(B)\",\n        \"∑_{i=1}^{n} i = n(n+1)/2\",\n    ],\n}\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# Entropy proxy (same formula as v6)\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\n\ndef activation_entropy(x: torch.Tensor) -> float:\n    \"\"\"Estimate entropy of activation tensor via log-variance proxy.\n\n    Uses mean per-feature variance across batch and sequence as a\n    proxy for the information content of the representation.\n\n    Same formula as VSMLMV6._activation_entropy:\n        h = log(mean(var_per_feature) + eps)\n\n    Args:\n        x: (B, L, D) activation tensor\n\n    Returns:\n        Scalar entropy estimate (higher = more information content)\n    \"\"\"\n    # x shape: (B, L, D) — variance per feature across batch+seq\n    var_per_feat = x.var(dim=(0, 1))  # (D,)\n    mean_var = var_per_feat.mean()\n    return float(torch.log(mean_var + 1e-10).item())\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# Layer-by-layer residual capture with hooks\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\n\ndef capture_layer_entropies(\n    model, tokenizer, text: str\n) -> dict:\n    \"\"\"Capture pre- and post-layer entropy for every transformer layer.\n\n    Hooks the residual stream at each layer boundary. For GPTNeoX (Pythia),\n    each layer receives the residual stream as input and outputs the\n    updated residual stream.\n\n    Returns dict with:\n        embeddings_h: entropy of embedding output (before any layer)\n        layers: list of {layer, h_in, h_out, ratio, phi_dev}\n        loss: cross-entropy loss on the input\n    \"\"\"\n    from verbum.instrument import _get_layers\n\n    layers = _get_layers(model)\n    n_layers = len(layers)\n\n    # Storage for pre/post layer activations\n    pre_layer = {}   # layer_idx → entropy\n    post_layer = {}  # layer_idx → entropy\n\n    hooks = []\n\n    def make_pre_hook(layer_idx):\n        def hook_fn(module, args):\n            # args[0] is the hidden_states input\n            hidden = args[0] if isinstance(args[0], torch.Tensor) else args[0][0]\n            pre_layer[layer_idx] = activation_entropy(hidden)\n        return hook_fn\n\n    def make_post_hook(layer_idx):\n        def hook_fn(module, args, output):\n            hidden = output[0] if isinstance(output, tuple) else output\n            post_layer[layer_idx] = activation_entropy(hidden)\n        return hook_fn\n\n    try:\n        for i, layer in enumerate(layers):\n            hooks.append(layer.register_forward_pre_hook(make_pre_hook(i)))\n            hooks.append(layer.register_forward_hook(make_post_hook(i)))\n\n        inputs = tokenizer(text, return_tensors=\"pt\").to(model.device)\n        with torch.no_grad():\n            outputs = model(**inputs, labels=inputs[\"input_ids\"])\n\n        loss = outputs.loss.item() if outputs.loss is not None else None\n\n    finally:\n        for h in hooks:\n            h.remove()\n\n    # Build per-layer results\n    layer_results = []\n    for i in range(n_layers):\n        h_in = pre_layer.get(i, 0.0)\n        h_out = post_layer.get(i, 0.0)\n\n        if abs(h_in) > 1e-10:\n            ratio = h_out / h_in\n        else:\n            ratio = 1.0\n\n        layer_results.append({\n            \"layer\": i,\n            \"h_in\": round(h_in, 6),\n            \"h_out\": round(h_out, 6),\n            \"ratio\": round(ratio, 6),\n            \"phi_dev\": round(abs(ratio - INV_PHI), 6),\n            \"direction\": \"compressing\" if ratio < 1.0 else \"expanding\",\n        })\n\n    return {\n        \"layers\": layer_results,\n        \"loss\": loss,\n    }\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# Stratified analysis\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\n\ndef run_stratum(model, tokenizer, samples: list[str]) -> dict:\n    \"\"\"Run φ-compression analysis on a list of samples.\n\n    Returns summary with per-layer averages and aggregate stats.\n    \"\"\"\n    all_layer_data = {}  # layer_idx → {h_in: [...], h_out: [...], ratio: [...]}\n    all_losses = []\n\n    for text in samples:\n        result = capture_layer_entropies(model, tokenizer, text)\n        if result[\"loss\"] is not None:\n            all_losses.append(result[\"loss\"])\n\n        for lr in result[\"layers\"]:\n            idx = lr[\"layer\"]\n            if idx not in all_layer_data:\n                all_layer_data[idx] = {\"h_in\": [], \"h_out\": [], \"ratio\": []}\n            all_layer_data[idx][\"h_in\"].append(lr[\"h_in\"])\n            all_layer_data[idx][\"h_out\"].append(lr[\"h_out\"])\n            all_layer_data[idx][\"ratio\"].append(lr[\"ratio\"])\n\n    # Summarize per layer\n    layer_summary = []\n    all_ratios = []\n    for idx in sorted(all_layer_data.keys()):\n        d = all_layer_data[idx]\n        mean_ratio = np.mean(d[\"ratio\"])\n        std_ratio = np.std(d[\"ratio\"])\n        mean_h_in = np.mean(d[\"h_in\"])\n        mean_h_out = np.mean(d[\"h_out\"])\n        phi_dev = abs(mean_ratio - INV_PHI)\n        all_ratios.append(mean_ratio)\n\n        layer_summary.append({\n            \"layer\": idx,\n            \"mean_h_in\": round(float(mean_h_in), 6),\n            \"mean_h_out\": round(float(mean_h_out), 6),\n            \"mean_ratio\": round(float(mean_ratio), 6),\n            \"std_ratio\": round(float(std_ratio), 6),\n            \"phi_dev\": round(float(phi_dev), 6),\n            \"direction\": \"compressing\" if mean_ratio < 1.0 else \"expanding\",\n        })\n\n    # Aggregate\n    aggregate = {}\n    if all_ratios:\n        aggregate = {\n            \"mean_ratio\": round(float(np.mean(all_ratios)), 6),\n            \"std_ratio\": round(float(np.std(all_ratios)), 6),\n            \"mean_phi_dev\": round(float(np.mean([abs(r - INV_PHI) for r in all_ratios])), 6),\n            \"min_phi_dev\": round(float(np.min([abs(r - INV_PHI) for r in all_ratios])), 6),\n            \"closest_layer\": int(np.argmin([abs(r - INV_PHI) for r in all_ratios])),\n            \"target\": INV_PHI,\n        }\n\n    loss_summary = {}\n    if all_losses:\n        mean_loss = np.mean(all_losses)\n        loss_summary = {\n            \"mean_loss\": round(float(mean_loss), 4),\n            \"ppl\": round(float(np.exp(mean_loss)), 2),\n        }\n\n    return {\n        \"layers\": layer_summary,\n        \"aggregate\": aggregate,\n        \"loss\": loss_summary,\n    }\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# Multi-layer grouping (analogy to v6 passes)\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\n\ndef compute_pass_analogy(layer_summary: list[dict], n_layers: int) -> dict:\n    \"\"\"Group layers into thirds and compute per-group compression.\n\n    Pythia has 12 layers. Grouping into thirds (0-3, 4-7, 8-11)\n    provides an analogy to v6's ascending/apex/descending structure.\n\n    Also computes cumulative compression: the product of ratios\n    across a group of layers, giving the total compression factor.\n    \"\"\"\n    third = n_layers // 3\n    groups = {\n        \"early (L0-L3)\": list(range(0, third)),\n        \"middle (L4-L7)\": list(range(third, 2 * third)),\n        \"late (L8-L11)\": list(range(2 * third, n_layers)),\n    }\n\n    group_results = {}\n    for gname, glayers in groups.items():\n        ratios = [layer_summary[i][\"mean_ratio\"] for i in glayers if i < len(layer_summary)]\n        if ratios:\n            # Cumulative compression = product of ratios\n            cumulative = float(np.prod(ratios))\n            group_results[gname] = {\n                \"mean_ratio\": round(float(np.mean(ratios)), 6),\n                \"cumulative_compression\": round(cumulative, 6),\n                \"phi_dev\": round(float(abs(np.mean(ratios) - INV_PHI)), 6),\n                \"layers\": glayers,\n            }\n\n    # Total compression: embedding → final layer\n    all_ratios = [ls[\"mean_ratio\"] for ls in layer_summary]\n    total_compression = float(np.prod(all_ratios)) if all_ratios else 1.0\n\n    # Does the total compression approach 1/φ^n for some n?\n    # If each layer independently compresses at 1/φ, total = (1/φ)^n_layers\n    expected_phi_total = INV_PHI ** n_layers\n    total_phi_dev = abs(total_compression - expected_phi_total)\n\n    return {\n        \"groups\": group_results,\n        \"total_compression\": round(total_compression, 6),\n        \"expected_phi_total\": round(expected_phi_total, 10),\n        \"total_phi_dev\": round(total_phi_dev, 6),\n    }\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# Consecutive-layer pair analysis\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\n\ndef compute_layer_pairs(layer_summary: list[dict]) -> list[dict]:\n    \"\"\"Compute compression ratios for consecutive layer PAIRS.\n\n    If individual layers don't show φ-compression, maybe pairs of\n    layers (attention + FFN as a unit) do? This tests whether the\n    compression unit is larger than a single transformer layer.\n    \"\"\"\n    pairs = []\n    for i in range(0, len(layer_summary) - 1, 2):\n        l1 = layer_summary[i]\n        l2 = layer_summary[i + 1]\n\n        # Combined ratio = product of individual ratios\n        combined = l1[\"mean_ratio\"] * l2[\"mean_ratio\"]\n        phi_dev = abs(combined - INV_PHI)\n\n        pairs.append({\n            \"layers\": f\"L{l1['layer']}-L{l2['layer']}\",\n            \"ratio_1\": l1[\"mean_ratio\"],\n            \"ratio_2\": l2[\"mean_ratio\"],\n            \"combined_ratio\": round(float(combined), 6),\n            \"phi_dev\": round(float(phi_dev), 6),\n        })\n\n    return pairs\n\n\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n# Display\n# ══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════\n\n\ndef print_results(\n    overall: dict,\n    strata: dict[str, dict],\n    passes: dict,\n    pairs: list[dict],\n    n_layers: int,\n    verbose: bool = False,\n):\n    print(\"\\n\" + \"=\" * 70)\n    print(f\"  Pythia-160M φ-Compression Analysis\")\n    print(f\"  Target: 1/φ = {INV_PHI:.4f}\")\n    print(\"=\" * 70)\n\n    # ── Per-layer table ───────────────────────────────────────\n    print(f\"\\n  Per-layer compression:\")\n    print(f\"  {'Layer':>5} {'h_in':>8} {'h_out':>8} {'ratio':>8} {'±std':>8} {'φ-dev':>8}\")\n    print(f\"  {'─'*5} {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8}\")\n\n    for ls in overall[\"layers\"]:\n        marker = \" ←φ\" if ls[\"phi_dev\"] < 0.05 else \"\"\n        print(\n            f\"  L{ls['layer']:>3} {ls['mean_h_in']:>8.3f} {ls['mean_h_out']:>8.3f} \"\n            f\"{ls['mean_ratio']:>8.4f} {ls['std_ratio']:>8.4f} \"\n            f\"{ls['phi_dev']:>8.4f}{marker}\"\n        )\n\n    agg = overall[\"aggregate\"]\n    print(f\"  {'─'*5} {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8}\")\n    print(f\"  {'MEAN':>5} {'':>8} {'':>8} {agg['mean_ratio']:>8.4f} {agg['std_ratio']:>8.4f} {agg['mean_phi_dev']:>8.4f}\")\n    print(f\"  Closest to φ: layer {agg['closest_layer']} (dev={agg['min_phi_dev']:.4f})\")\n\n    # ── Layer pair analysis ───────────────────────────────────\n    if pairs:\n        print(f\"\\n  Layer-pair compression (attention+FFN as unit):\")\n        print(f\"  {'Pair':>8} {'r1':>8} {'r2':>8} {'combined':>8} {'φ-dev':>8}\")\n        print(f\"  {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8}\")\n        for p in pairs:\n            marker = \" ←φ\" if p[\"phi_dev\"] < 0.05 else \"\"\n            print(\n                f\"  {p['layers']:>8} {p['ratio_1']:>8.4f} {p['ratio_2']:>8.4f} \"\n                f\"{p['combined_ratio']:>8.4f} {p['phi_dev']:>8.4f}{marker}\"\n            )\n\n    # ── Layer group analysis (v6 analogy) ─────────────────────\n    if passes and \"groups\" in passes:\n        print(f\"\\n  Layer groups (analogy to v6 ascending/apex/descending):\")\n        print(f\"  {'Group':>20} {'mean_ratio':>10} {'cumulative':>10} {'φ-dev':>8}\")\n        print(f\"  {'─'*20} {'─'*10} {'─'*10} {'─'*8}\")\n        for gname, gdata in passes[\"groups\"].items():\n            marker = \" ←φ\" if gdata[\"phi_dev\"] < 0.05 else \"\"\n            print(\n                f\"  {gname:>20} {gdata['mean_ratio']:>10.4f} \"\n                f\"{gdata['cumulative_compression']:>10.4f} \"\n                f\"{gdata['phi_dev']:>8.4f}{marker}\"\n            )\n        print(f\"\\n  Total compression (all layers): {passes['total_compression']:.6f}\")\n        print(f\"  Expected if each layer ≡ 1/φ:   {passes['expected_phi_total']:.10f}\")\n\n    # ── Per-stratum table ─────────────────────────────────────\n    print(f\"\\n  Per-stratum compression:\")\n    print(f\"  {'stratum':>15} {'mean_ratio':>10} {'φ-dev':>8} {'loss':>8} {'ppl':>8}\")\n    print(f\"  {'─'*15} {'─'*10} {'─'*8} {'─'*8} {'─'*8}\")\n\n    stratum_means = []\n    for sname in [\"prose\", \"compositional\", \"technical\", \"math\"]:\n        if sname not in strata:\n            continue\n        ssummary = strata[sname]\n        agg = ssummary[\"aggregate\"]\n        loss = ssummary.get(\"loss\", {})\n        mr = agg[\"mean_ratio\"]\n        pd = agg[\"mean_phi_dev\"]\n        stratum_means.append(mr)\n        ml = loss.get(\"mean_loss\", 0)\n        ppl = loss.get(\"ppl\", 0)\n        print(f\"  {sname:>15} {mr:>10.4f} {pd:>8.4f} {ml:>8.3f} {ppl:>8.1f}\")\n\n    if len(stratum_means) >= 2:\n        spread = max(stratum_means) - min(stratum_means)\n        print(f\"  {'─'*15} {'─'*10} {'─'*8}\")\n        print(f\"  {'spread':>15} {spread:>10.4f}\")\n        if spread < 0.01:\n            print(f\"  ✓ Content-independent compression — universal pattern.\")\n        elif spread < 0.05:\n            print(f\"  → Near content-independent. ## First, honesty about what we actually know

So "from what we have seen" is external lore, not our own measurement — the lore is real (published weight-diff and lens studies do tend to find post-training changes concentrated late and in narrow directions), but we haven't measured it ourselves. Conservative claims: it's a plausible prior, not an established fact here.

## What the confound does and doesn't touch

Qwen3-4B is the post-trained release. So the concern decomposes cleanly:

**Not threatened:** the *measurement*. The depth flip is real wherever it came from — recency through the stack, sign flip at L34/35, replicated exactly. Location is licensed.

**Threatened:** the *reading*. I framed it as "the model assembles its decision at the top" — native physics. Your alternative: the base model is a recency-tracker all the way through (evidence integrator, matching the commutative weight face), and **primacy is an installed behavior** — RLHF/instruct tuning bolted a "commit to what was asserted first" policy onto the last two layers. Under that reading:

- The depth law isn't "where decisions form" — it's "where post-training lives."
- More importantly, **s328's EARLY-COMMITMENT win itself requalifies**: the tape-side primacy would be an alignment artifact, not a property of the compiled type system. That's a real threat to how we've been reading the §Synthesis spine on the tape.
- It would also *cohere* suspiciously well with our behavior-is-tape-resident thread — installed behaviors riding late layers over an unchanged evidence substrate.

## The good news: this is cheaply decidable

`Qwen/Qwen3-4B-Base` exists — same lineage, pre-post-training. The harness takes `--model-id`; the run is ~3 minutes. Three clean outcomes:

1. **Base shows the same late-layer primacy flip** → the depth law is pretraining-native; your confound is excluded on this lineage.
2. **Base reads recency everywhere** (no flip, or L-final recency/ns) → primacy is **post-training-installed**. Frame-damaging in the best way — it would be a pre-registered discovery that the "decision" register is an alignment overlay.
3. **Attenuated/mixed** → partial installation; dose reading.

Either way we learn something we currently can't distinguish. It owes its own pre-reg (this is now a post-hoc hypothesis — your observation came after the data, so it needs a frozen a-priori before we run).

### Thinking

The test harness ran successfully and returned a verdict I should ignore due to the freeze, so I'm applying the PV tree from the results.json instead. Looking at the PV0 anchor settings, it's marked as True with a real margin of 2.933 and identity at 0.0000, but I need to verify the standing value since the earlier OR0 pass failed due to replication issues — the anchor=True configuration should include standing greater than zero.

For PV1, the base D_L(final) is −0.090 with primacy p=0.837 (not significant), and the recency tail p is approximately 0.163 (also not significant), which means D_L(final) is absent in the base with an a-priori of 30, though I need to note the power caveat.

PV2 shows the advisory metrics: A_LL_band is −0.824 with p=0.0001, indicating strong recency substrate in the base that's actually stronger than what appears in the instruct version, while D_T_band is −3.747 showing even deeper recency. The commit_layer is None for both instruments, meaning there's no point where the order sign flips positive and persists, and the primacy flip at L34/35 doesn't exist in the base — so the clearest picture from the freeze is this shared recency substrate with differing tops, exactly as it landed.

For OR3, the kernel is not significant in the base (+0.005 p=0.49), and the slot curve [−0.049, 0.329, 0.488, −0.054] shows that the leading-anti also crashes in the base, unlike in instruct where it was repaired to 0.386 — so the base has no primacy recovery mechanism at the final layer.

I need to check results.json for the recency tail p-value and standing_LL to finalize the interpretation. The key insight is that post-training appears to have installed the late-layer primacy commitment as an overlay on top of the native recency tracking substrate, which means s328's early commitment is actually a behavior of the deployed model rather than something inherent to the base. Michael's confound holds up in its "absent" form — the mechanism was created, not inverted.…
